Cost Optimisation of Individual-Based Institutional Reward Incentives for Promoting Cooperation in Finite Populations
Authors: M. H. Duong, C. M. Durbac, T. A. Han
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
In this paper, we study the problem of cost optimisation of individual-based institutional incentives (reward, punishment, and hybrid) for guaranteeing a certain minimal level of cooperative behaviour in a well-mixed, finite population. In this scheme, the individuals in the population interact via cooperation dilemmas (Donation Game or Public Goods Game) in which institutional reward is carried out only if cooperation is not abundant enough (i.e., the number of cooperators is below a threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le t\le N-1$$\end{document}1≤t≤N-1, where N is the population size); and similarly, institutional punishment is carried out only when defection is too abundant. We study analytically the cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1 for the reward incentive under the small mutation limit assumption and two different initial states, showing that the cost function is always non-decreasing. We derive the neutral drift and strong selection limits when the intensity of selection tends to zero and infinity, respectively. We numerically investigate the problem for other values of t and for population dynamics with arbitrary mutation rates.

Introduction

Cooperation refers to the act of paying a cost to oneself in order to convey a benefit to somebody else. It is one of the cornerstones of human civilisation and one of the reasons for our unprecedented success as a species (Atkins et al. 2019). Organisations are constantly faced with the problem of allocating resources in a budget-effective way. This issue becomes particularly essential for institutions like local governments and the United Nations, where the optimisation of resources is crucial in facilitating cooperative endeavors. Given the paramount importance of cooperation, these organisations are tasked with strategically managing the costs associated with incentivising collective efforts (Ostrom 2005; Van Lange et al. 2014).

A well-established theoretical framework for analysing the promotion of cooperation is Evolutionary Game Theory (EGT) (Sigmund 2010), which has been used in both deterministic and stochastic settings. Using this framework, several mechanisms for promoting the evolution of cooperation have been studied including kin selection, direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, network reciprocity, group selection, and different forms of incentives (Nowak 2006b; Sigmund 2010; Perc et al. 2017; Rand and Nowak 2013; Van Lange et al. 2014; Xia et al. 2023; Liwen et al. 2020; Capraro and Perc 2021).

The current work focuses on institutional incentives (Sasaki et al. 2012; Sigmund et al. 2010; Wang et al. 2019; Duong and Han 2021; Cimpeanu et al. 2021; Sun et al. 2021; Van Lange et al. 2014; Gürerk et al. 2006; Góis et al. 2019; Sun et al. 2021; Liu and Chen 2022; Flores and Han 2024; Wang et al. 2024; Hua and Liu 2024), which are a plan of action involving the use of reward (i.e., increasing the payoff of cooperators), punishment (i.e., decreasing the payoff of defectors), or a combination of the two, by an external decision-maker. More precisely, we study how the aforementioned institutional incentives can be used in a cost-efficient way for maximising the levels of cooperative behaviour in a population of self-regarding individuals. In the literature, although there is a significant amount of works using agent-based numerical simulations, there are only a few papers that employ a rigorous analysis of the problem at hand (Wang et al. 2019; Han and Tran-Thanh 2018; Duong and Han 2021; Duong et al. 2023; Wang et al. 2022, 2023). The works that do use analysis employ two complementary approaches, either in a continuous setting where the evolutionary processes are modelled as a continuous dynamical system (for instance, using the replicator dynamics) (Wang et al. 2019, 2022, 2023) or in a discrete setting, in which the population dynamics is modelled as a Markov chain (Han and Tran-Thanh 2018; Duong and Han 2021; Duong et al. 2023). We review in more detail both approaches in the next paragraphs since they are most relevant to the present work.

In the discrete setting, the evolutionary process is often described by a Markov chain with an update rule (for instance, in the absence of mutation, it is an imitation process with the Fermi strategy update rule). For well-mixed finite populations and general two-player two-strategy games, the problem of promoting the evolution of cooperative behaviour with a minimum cost is formulated and numerically studied in Han and Tran-Thanh (2018). In this paper, the decision-maker may use a full-invest scheme, in which, in each generation, all cooperators are rewarded (or all defectors are punished) or an individual-based scheme, in which cooperators (defectors) are rewarded (punished, respectively) only if cooperation is not frequent enough (defection is too abundant, respectively). In both cases, the expected total cost of interference is a finite sum, over the state space of the Markov chain, of per generation costs. In Duong and Han (2021), the authors then analyse the cost function for a full-invest scheme in which individuals interact via donation games or public goods games. They prove that the cost function exhibits a phase transition when the intensity of selection varies and exactly calculate the optimal cost of the incentive for any given intensity of selection. In a more recent paper (Duong et al. 2023), similar results are obtained in the case of hybrid (mixed) reward and punishment incentives.

In the continuous setting, the evolutionary process is modelled by the replicator dynamics, which is a set of differential equations describing the evolution of the behavioural frequencies. For infinitely large, well-mixed populations, the problem of providing a minimum cost that guarantees a sufficient level of cooperation is formulated as an optimal control problem in Wang et al. (2019). By using the approach of the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation, the authors theoretically obtain the optimal reward (positive) or punishment (negative) incentive strategies with the minimal cumulative cost, respectively. Similar results for structured population are obtained in Wang et al. (2023) (for either reward or punish incentive separately) and in Wang et al. (2023) (for combined incentives), using pair approximate methods. In these papers, the decision-maker implements incentives centrally within the whole population. In Wang et al. (2022), the authors consider the same problem but using decentralised incentives, that is, in each game group, a local incentive-providing institution implements local punishment or reward incentives on group members. We also refer the reader to Wang et al. (2021) for a recent survey on this optimal control approach.

Overview of contribution of this paper. Following the discrete approach in Han and Tran-Thanh (2018); Duong and Han (2021); Duong et al. (2023), in this work, we rigorously study the problem of cost optimisation of institutional reward or/and punishment for maximising the levels of cooperative behaviour (or guaranteeing at least a certain level of cooperation) for well-mixed, finite populations. We focus on individual-based schemes, in which, in each generation, only when the number of cooperators/defectors is below/above a certain threshold t (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le t< N-1$$\end{document}1≤t<N-1, where N is the population size), are they rewarded/punished, respectively (the case of the full-invest scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=N-1$$\end{document}t=N-1 has been studied in Duong and Han (2021), Duong et al. (2023)).

Analysing this problem for an arbitrary value of t would be very challenging due to the number of parameters involved such as the number of individuals in the population, the strength of selection, the game-specific quantities, as well as the efficiency ratios of providing the corresponding incentive. In particular, the Markov chain based evolutionary process is of order equal to the population size, which is large but finite. The calculation of the entries of the corresponding fundamental matrix, which appear in the cost function, is intricate, both analytically and computationally.

Our present work provides a rigorous analysis of this problem in the case of reward for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1. The main analytical results of the paper can be summarised as follows. (i)We show that the cost function is always non-decreasing regardless of the values of other parameters, for two initial state assumptions, namely when the evolutionary dynamics starts either equally from the two homogeneous states or from the all-defector homogeneous state. The monotonicity of the reward cost function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r$$\end{document}Er with respect to the incentive cost per capita \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ highlights that, in order to achieve a higher level of cooperation, the institution needs to employ more financial resources.(ii)We obtain the asymptotic behaviour of the cost function in the limits of neutral drift and strong selection when the intensity of selection tends to zero or infinity, respectively.We also numerically investigate the cost function and its behaviour for other values of t. While the main analytical results focus on the small mutation limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}μ→0, we perform numerical simulations for the case of arbitrary mutation rates.

(i)We show that the cost function is always non-decreasing regardless of the values of other parameters, for two initial state assumptions, namely when the evolutionary dynamics starts either equally from the two homogeneous states or from the all-defector homogeneous state. The monotonicity of the reward cost function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r$$\end{document}Er with respect to the incentive cost per capita \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ highlights that, in order to achieve a higher level of cooperation, the institution needs to employ more financial resources.

We show that the cost function is always non-decreasing regardless of the values of other parameters, for two initial state assumptions, namely when the evolutionary dynamics starts either equally from the two homogeneous states or from the all-defector homogeneous state. The monotonicity of the reward cost function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r$$\end{document}Er with respect to the incentive cost per capita \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ highlights that, in order to achieve a higher level of cooperation, the institution needs to employ more financial resources.

We obtain the asymptotic behaviour of the cost function in the limits of neutral drift and strong selection when the intensity of selection tends to zero or infinity, respectively.

The rest of the paper is organised as follows. In Sect. 2, we present the model and methods. Our main results are Theorems 2 and 3 on the monotonicity of the reward cost function for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1 and Propositions 1, 2, 3, and 4 on the asymptotic behaviour (neutral drift, strong selection limits) of the reward cost function for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1, in Sect. 3. Section 4 contains the cost function (for reward, punishment, and hybrid incentives) in the case of a general mutation rate. In Sect. 5, we provide a numerical analysis of the reward cost function for both the small mutation limit and the general mutation rate cases, highlighting its qualitative behaviour. Summary and further discussions are provided in Sect. 6. Finally, Sect. 1 contains small population computations, detailed calculations for obtaining the reward cost function for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 2$$\end{document}t=2, as well as numerical simulations for the punishment and hybrid cost functions.

Model and Methods

In this section, we present the model and methods of the paper. We first introduce the class of games, namely cooperation dilemmas, that we are interested in throughout this work.

Evolutionary Processes

We consider an evolutionary process of a well-mixed, finite population of N interacting individuals (players) and we model the finite population dynamics on an absorbing Markov chain of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N+1)$$\end{document}(N+1) states, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{S_0,..., S_N\}$$\end{document}{S0,...,SN}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_j$$\end{document}Sj represents a population with j cooperators (and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-j$$\end{document}N-j defectors) (the sates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN are absorbing). We employ the Fermi strategy update rule (Traulsen and Nowak 2006) stating that a player X with fitness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_X$$\end{document}fX adopts the strategy of another player Y with fitness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_Y$$\end{document}fY with a probability given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{X,Y}=\left( 1 + e^{-\beta (f_Y-f_X)}\right) ^{-1}$$\end{document}PX,Y=1+e-β(fY-fX)-1, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β represents the intensity of selection.

Cooperation Dilemmas

Individuals engage with one another using one of the following one-shot (i.e., non-repeated) cooperation dilemmas: the Donation Game (DG) or its multi-player version, the Public Goods Game (PGG). Strategy wise, each player can choose to either cooperate (C) or defect (D).

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _C(j)$$\end{document}ΠC(j) be the average payoff of a C player (cooperator) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _D(j)$$\end{document}ΠD(j) that of a D player (defector), in a population with j C players and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N-j)$$\end{document}(N-j) D players. As can be seen below, the difference in payoffs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta = \Pi _C(j) - \Pi _D(j)$$\end{document}δ=ΠC(j)-ΠD(j) in both games does not depend on j. For the two cooperation dilemmas considered in this paper, namely the Donation Games and the Public Goods Games, it is always the case that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta < 0$$\end{document}δ<0. This does not cover some weak social dilemmas such as the snowdrift game, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0 for some j, the general prisoner’s dilemma, and the collective risk game (Sun et al. 2021), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ depends on j.

Donation Game (DG)

The Donation Game is a form of Prisoner’s Dilemma in which cooperation corresponds to offering the other player a benefit B at a personal cost c, satisfying that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B > c$$\end{document}B>c. Defection means offering nothing. The payoff matrix of DG (for the row player) is given as followsDenoting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{X,Y}$$\end{document}πX,Y the payoff of a strategist X when playing with a strategist Y from the payoff matrix above, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Pi _C(j)&=\frac{(j-1)\pi _{C,C} + (N-j)\pi _{C,D}}{N-1} = \frac{(j-1) (B-c) + (N-j) (-c)}{N-1},\\ \Pi _D(j)&=\frac{j\pi _{D,C} + (N-j-1)\pi _{D,D}}{N-1} =\frac{j B}{N-1}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΠC(j)=(j-1)πC,C+(N-j)πC,DN-1=(j-1)(B-c)+(N-j)(-c)N-1,ΠD(j)=jπD,C+(N-j-1)πD,DN-1=jBN-1.Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta = \Pi _C(j) - \Pi _D(j) = -\Big (c + \frac{B}{N-1}\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δ=ΠC(j)-ΠD(j)=-(c+BN-1).

Public Goods Game (PGG)

In a Public Goods Game, players interact in a group of size n, where they decide to cooperate, contributing an amount \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c > 0$$\end{document}c>0 to a common pool, or to defect, contributing nothing to the pool. The total contribution in a group is multiplied by a factor r, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1< r < n$$\end{document}1<r<n (for the PGG to be a social dilemma), which is then shared equally among all members of the group, regardless of their strategy. Intuitively, contributing nothing offers one a higher amount of money after redistribution.

The average payoffs, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _C(j)$$\end{document}ΠC(j) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _D(j)$$\end{document}ΠD(j), are calculated based on the assumption that the groups engaging in a public goods game are given by multivariate hypergeometric sampling. Thereby, for transitions between two pure states, this reduces to sampling, without replacement, from a hypergeometric distribution. More precisely, we obtain (Hauert et al. 2007)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Pi _C(j)&= \sum ^{n-1}_{i=0}\frac{\genfrac(){0.0pt}0{j-1}{i}\genfrac(){0.0pt}0{N-j}{n-1-i}}{ \genfrac(){0.0pt}0{N-1}{n-1}} \ \left( \frac{(i+1)rc}{n} - c\right) \\&=\frac{rc}{n}\left( 1 + (j-1)\frac{n-1}{N-1}\right) - c,\\ \Pi _D(j)&=\sum ^{n-1}_{i=0}\frac{\genfrac(){0.0pt}0{j}{i}\genfrac(){0.0pt}0{N-1-j}{n-1-i}}{ \genfrac(){0.0pt}0{N-1}{n-1}} \ \frac{jrc}{n} =\frac{rc(n-1)}{n(N-1)}j. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΠC(j)=∑i=0n-1j-1iN-jn-1-iN-1n-1(i+1)rcn-c=rcn1+(j-1)n-1N-1-c,ΠD(j)=∑i=0n-1jiN-1-jn-1-iN-1n-1jrcn=rc(n-1)n(N-1)j.Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta = \Pi _C(j) - \Pi _D(j) = -c \left( 1 - \frac{r(N-n)}{n(N-1)} \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δ=ΠC(j)-ΠD(j)=-c1-r(N-n)n(N-1).

Cost of Institutional Incentives

To reward a cooperator (to punish a defector), the institution has to pay an amount \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta /a$$\end{document}θ/a (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta /b$$\end{document}θ/b, respectively) so that the cooperator’s (defector’s) payoff increases (decreases) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a, b > 0$$\end{document}a,b>0 are constants representing the efficiency ratios of providing this type of incentive.

In an institutional enforcement setting, we assume that the institution has full information about the population composition or statistics at the time of decision-making. That is, given the well-mixed population setting, we assume that the number j of cooperators in the population is known. While reward and punishment and their usefulness as institutional incentives have been previously studied, including in Duong and Han (2021), Duong et al. (2023) which are most relevant to this work, the approach in the aforementioned papers is a ‘full-invest’ one, i.e., in each state of the evolutionary process, all cooperators (defectors) are rewarded (punished).

In the present paper, we consider investment schemes that reward (or/and punish) t C players (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N - t$$\end{document}N-t D players) whenever the number of cooperators in the population does not exceed a given threshold t (whenever the number of defectors in the population exceeds a given threshold t) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t \le N-1$$\end{document}1≤t≤N-1. The argument for this type of approach and its applicability in real life is as follows. Firstly, if cooperation is sufficiently frequent, the cooperators might survive by themselves without further need of costly incentives. Secondly, if the institution spreads their incentive budget for too many individuals, then the impact on each individual might not be enough to alter the global dynamics.

Hence, we have, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le j\le t$$\end{document}1≤j≤t, the cost per generation for the incentive providing institution is1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _j = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{j}{a}\theta ,\quad \text {reward incentive},\\ \frac{N-j}{b}\theta ,\quad \text {punishment incentive},\\ \min \Big (\frac{j}{a}, \frac{N-j}{b}\Big )\theta ,\quad \text {mixed incentive}, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θj=jaθ,reward incentive,N-jbθ,punishment incentive,min(ja,N-jb)θ,mixed incentive,while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _j= 0$$\end{document}θj=0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t<j\le N-1$$\end{document}t<j≤N-1.

Next, we derive the total expected cost of inference over all generations. We do so for two different initial states: i) randomly commencing in the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 or the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN and ii) starting in the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 (as it is more likely to find the population at the homogeneous state of all defectors than that of all cooperators).

We firstly study i), i.e., the population is equally likely to start in the homogeneous state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 (no cooperators) as well as in the homogeneous state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN (all cooperators). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n_{ik})_{i,k=1}^{N-1}$$\end{document}(nik)i,k=1N-1 be the entries of the fundamental matrix of the absorbing Markov chain of the evolutionary process. The entries give the expected number of times the population is in the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_j$$\end{document}Sj if it has started in the transient state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i$$\end{document}Si (Kemeny 1976). Under the above assumption, a mutant can randomly equally occur either at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN. Thus, the expected number of visits at state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_j$$\end{document}Sj (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le j\le N-1$$\end{document}1≤j≤N-1) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2} (n_{1j} + n_{N-1,j})$$\end{document}12(n1j+nN-1,j). Therefore, the expected cost of inference over all generations is given by2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E(\theta )=\frac{1}{2}\sum _{j=1}^{N-1} (n_{1j}+n_{N-1,j})\theta _j=\frac{1}{2}\sum _{j=1}^t (n_{1j}+n_{N-1,j})\theta _j, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(θ)=12∑j=1N-1(n1j+nN-1,j)θj=12∑j=1t(n1j+nN-1,j)θj,where the second equality follows from the individual-based incentives (1).

Remark

We comment on our assumption that the population is equally likely to start in either homogeneous state. This assumption is reasonable when mutation is negligible and is often made in many works based on agent-based simulations (Chen and Perc 2014; Cimpeanu et al. 2023; Szolnoki and Perc Oct 2017; Han et al. 2018; Sasaki et al. 2015) (in these works, simulations end whenever the population fixates in a homogeneous state). Our model therefore encapsulates the intermediate-run dynamics, an approximation that is valid if the time-scale is long enough for one type to reach fixation, but too short for the next mutant to appear. It might thus be more practically useful for the optimisation of the institutional budget for providing incentives on an intermediate timescale.

In the most general case, when mutation is frequent, the above assumption might not be suitable. For example, if cooperators are very likely to fixate in a population of defectors, but defectors are unlikely to fixate in a population of cooperators, mutants are on average more likely to appear in the homogeneous cooperative population (that is in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN). Similarly, if defectors are very likely to fixate in a population of cooperators, but cooperators are unlikely to fixate in a population of defectors, mutants are on average more likely to appear in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 rather than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN. In general, in the long-run, the population will start at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = 0$$\end{document}i=0 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = N$$\end{document}i=N, respectively) with probability equal to the frequency of D (C) computed at the equilibrium, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_D = 1/(r+1)$$\end{document}fD=1/(r+1) (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_C = r/(r+1)$$\end{document}fC=r/(r+1), respectively), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = e^{\beta (N-1)(\delta + \theta )}$$\end{document}r=eβ(N-1)(δ+θ). Thus, generally, the expected number of visits at state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i$$\end{document}Si will be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f_D n_{1i} + f_C n_{N-1,i}$$\end{document}fDn1i+fCnN-1,i. The cost function will therefore be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r(\theta ) = \sum _{j=1}^{N-1} (f_D*n_{1,j} + f_C*n_{N-1,j} )*\theta _j. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er(θ)=∑j=1N-1(fD∗n1,j+fC∗nN-1,j)∗θj.We will study the general setting in future work.

We now compute the cost function for (ii), i.e., where the dynamics starts from the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 with no cooperators, which could be the case in the absence of incentives. Thus, the expected cost of inference over all generations is given by3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E(\theta )=\sum _{j=1}^{N-1} n_{1j}\theta _j=\sum _{j=1}^t n_{1j}\theta _j. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(θ)=∑j=1N-1n1jθj=∑j=1tn1jθj.

Cooperation Frequency and Optimal Incentives

Next, we construct the problem of cost optimisation of individual-based institutional incentives (reward, punishment, and hybrid) for maximising the level (or guaranteeing at least a certain level) of cooperative behaviour.

Since the population consists of only two strategies, the fixation probabilities of a C (D) player in a homogeneous population of D (C) players when the interference scheme is carried out are, respectively, Novak (2006a)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \rho _{D,C}&= \left( 1+\sum _{i = 1}^{N-1} \prod _{k = 1}^i \frac{1+e^{\beta (\Pi _C(k)-\Pi _D(k) + \delta (k) \theta )}}{1+e^{-\beta (\Pi _C(k)-\Pi _D(k)+ \delta (k)\theta )}} \right) ^{-1}, \\ \rho _{C,D}&= \left( 1+\sum _{i = 1}^{N-1} \prod _{k = 1}^i \frac{1+e^{\beta (\Pi _D(k)-\Pi _C(k) - \delta (k)\theta )}}{1+e^{-\beta (\Pi _D(k)-\Pi _C(k)-\delta (k)\theta )}} \right) ^{-1}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρD,C=1+∑i=1N-1∏k=1i1+eβ(ΠC(k)-ΠD(k)+δ(k)θ)1+e-β(ΠC(k)-ΠD(k)+δ(k)θ)-1,ρC,D=1+∑i=1N-1∏k=1i1+eβ(ΠD(k)-ΠC(k)-δ(k)θ)1+e-β(ΠD(k)-ΠC(k)-δ(k)θ)-1.where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta (k) = 1$$\end{document}δ(k)=1 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \le t$$\end{document}k≤t and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta (k) = 0$$\end{document}δ(k)=0 otherwise.

Computing the stationary distribution using these fixation probabilities, we obtain the frequency of cooperation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\rho _{D,C}}{\rho _{D,C}+\rho _{C,D}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρD,CρD,C+ρC,D.Hence, this frequency of cooperation can be maximised by maximising4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \max _{\theta } \left( \rho _{D,C}/\rho _{C,D}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}maxθρD,C/ρC,D.The fraction in Eq. (4) can be simplified as follows (Nowak 2006b)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\rho _{D,C}}{\rho _{C,D}}= & {} \prod _{k = 1}^{N-1} \frac{u_{i,i-1}}{u_{i,i+1}} =\prod _{k = 1}^{N-1} \frac{1 + e^{\beta [\Pi _C(k)-\Pi _D(k) + \delta (k) \theta ]}}{1 + e^{-\beta [\Pi _C(k)-\Pi _D(k) + \delta (k)\theta ]}} \\= & {} e^{\beta \sum _{k = 1}^{N-1} \left( \Pi _C(k)-\Pi _D(k) + \delta (k)\theta \right) } \\= & {} e^{\beta [(N-1) \delta + t \theta ]}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρD,CρC,D=∏k=1N-1ui,i-1ui,i+1=∏k=1N-11+eβ[ΠC(k)-ΠD(k)+δ(k)θ]1+e-β[ΠC(k)-ΠD(k)+δ(k)θ]=eβ∑k=1N-1ΠC(k)-ΠD(k)+δ(k)θ=eβ[(N-1)δ+tθ].In the above transformation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,i-1}$$\end{document}ui,i-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,i-1}$$\end{document}ui,i-1 are the probabilities to decrease or increase the number of C players (i.e., i) by one in each time step, respectively.

Under neutral selection (i.e., when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 0$$\end{document}β=0), there is no need to use incentives as no player is likely to copy another player and any changes in strategy that happen are due to noise as opposed to incentives. Thus, we only consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0. The goal is to ensure at least an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in [0,1]$$\end{document}ω∈[0,1] fraction of cooperation, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\rho _{D,C}}{\rho _{D,C}+\rho _{C,D}} \ge \omega $$\end{document}ρD,CρD,C+ρC,D≥ω. Thus, it follows from the equation above that5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta \ge \theta _0(\omega ) = \frac{1}{t}\left( \frac{1}{\beta }\log \left( \frac{\omega }{1-\omega }\right) - (N-1)\delta \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ≥θ0(ω)=1t1βlogω1-ω-(N-1)δ.It is guaranteed that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \ge \theta _0(\omega )$$\end{document}θ≥θ0(ω), at least an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω fraction of cooperation is expected in the long run. This condition implies that the lower bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ monotonically depends on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β. Namely, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \ge 0.5$$\end{document}ω≥0.5, it increases with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β and when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega < 0.5$$\end{document}ω<0.5, it decreases with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β.

Bringing everything together, we obtain the following constrained mathematical minimisation problem of individual-based institutional incentives (reward, punishment, and hybrid) guaranteeing at least a certain level of cooperative behaviour:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min _{\theta \ge \theta _0} E(\theta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}minθ≥θ0E(θ),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(\theta )$$\end{document}E(θ) can either be the reward (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ)), punishment (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_p(\theta )$$\end{document}Ep(θ)), or hybrid cost function (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{mix}(\theta )$$\end{document}Emix(θ)).

The main aim of this paper is study the above optimisation problem. We focus on the reward incentive, in which the decision-maker rewards j cooperators whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \le t$$\end{document}j≤t, as in (1) (thus, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1$$\end{document}a=1 in (1) throughout the paper since it does not affect our optimisation problem). In principle, the punishment and mixed incentives cases are similar, and we only provide numerical investigations for these schemes.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1, we analytically show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ) is non-decreasing as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ for all values of other parameters. We also establish the neutral and strong selection limits of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ), that is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\beta \rightarrow 0} E_r(\theta ) \quad \text {and}\quad \lim _{\beta \rightarrow \infty } E_r(\theta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→0Er(θ)andlimβ→∞Er(θ).For other values of t, we numerically investigate the properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ).

Reward Incentive Under a Small Mutation Limit

We first calculate the reward cost function, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ), more explicitly. To this end, we compute the expected number of times the population contains i C players for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le i \le N-1$$\end{document}1≤i≤N-1. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U = \{u_{ik}\}_{i,k = 1}^{N-1}$$\end{document}U={uik}i,k=1N-1 denote the transition matrix between the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-1$$\end{document}N-1 transient states, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{S_1,..., S_{N-1}\}$$\end{document}{S1,...,SN-1}. According to the Fermi update rule, the transition matrix is given as follows:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{i,i\pm k}= & {} 0 \qquad \text { for all } k \ge 2, \nonumber \\ u_{i,i\pm 1}= & {} \frac{N-i}{N} \frac{i}{N} \left( 1 + e^{\mp \beta [\Pi _C(i) - \Pi _D(i)+\theta _i/i]}\right) ^{-1},\nonumber \\ u_{i,i}= & {} 1 - u_{i,i+1} -u_{i,i-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ui,i±k=0for allk≥2,ui,i±1=N-iNiN1+e∓β[ΠC(i)-ΠD(i)+θi/i]-1,ui,i=1-ui,i+1-ui,i-1.For simplicity, we normalise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1$$\end{document}a=1, and obtain (recalling that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _C(i)-\Pi _D (i)=\delta $$\end{document}ΠC(i)-ΠD(i)=δ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le N-1$$\end{document}1≤i≤N-1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _i/i=\frac{\theta }{a}=\theta $$\end{document}θi/i=θa=θ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le t$$\end{document}1≤i≤t and zero otherwise):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{i,i\pm k}&=0 \quad \text {for}\quad k\ge 2, \\u_{i,i+1}&={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{(N-i)i}{N^2}\Big (1+e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta )}\Big )^{-1}\quad \text {for}\quad 1\le i\le t,\\ \frac{(N-i)i}{N^2}\Big (1+e^{-\beta \delta }\Big )^{-1}\quad \text {for}\quad t< i\le N-1,\\ \end{array}\right. } \\u_{i,i-1}&={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{(N-i)i}{N^2}\Big (1+e^{\beta (\delta +\theta )}\Big )^{-1}\quad \text {for}\quad 1\le By using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{1+m}+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{m}}=1$$\end{document}11+m+11+1m=1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=e^{\beta [\Pi _C(i) - \Pi _D(i)+\theta _i/i]}$$\end{document}m=eβ[ΠC(i)-ΠD(i)+θi/i], we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,i+1}+u_{i,i-1}=\frac{N-i}{N}\frac{i}{N}$$\end{document}ui,i+1+ui,i-1=N-iNiN. Therefore, the expected total cost of interference for reward in the case of the dynamics starting equally in either state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 or state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN is10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r(\theta )&= \frac{1}{2} \sum _{j=1}^{N-1}(n_{1j} + n_{N-1,j}) \theta _j \nonumber \\&=\frac{1}{2} \sum _{j=1}^{t}(n_{1j} + n_{N-1,j}) \theta _j \nonumber \\&= \frac{N^2\theta }{2} \sum _{j=1}^{t}\frac{(W^{-1})_{1,j} + (W^{-1})_{N-1,j}}{N-j}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er(θ)=12∑j=1N-1(n1j+nN-1,j)θj=12∑j=1t(n1j+nN-1,j)θj=N2θ2∑j=1t(W-1)1,j+(W-1)N-1,jN-j,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _j = \theta j$$\end{document}θj=θj follows from Eq. (2) normalised with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a = 1$$\end{document}a=1.

Similarly, when the population starts in the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0, we have11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r(\theta )&= \sum _{j=1}^{N-1}n_{1j}\theta _j=\sum _{j=1}^{t}n_{1j} \theta _j=\sum _{j=1}^{t}\frac{(W^{-1})_{1,j}}{N-j}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er(θ)=∑j=1N-1n1jθj=∑j=1tn1jθj=∑j=1t(W-1)1,jN-j.The advantage of expressing the cost function in terms of the entries of the matrix W is that this matrix is tri-diagonal. The inverse of a tri-diagonal matrix can be theoretically computed using recursive formulae, see e.g., Huang and McColl (1997). In general, these formulae are still very hard to analytically explore. However, in the special extreme cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=N-1$$\end{document}t=N-1, we can explicitly obtain the entries of the inverse matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{-1}$$\end{document}W-1. Therefore, we obtain analytically the explicit formula for the cost function. The case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=N-1$$\end{document}t=N-1 has already been studied in Duong and Han (2021), Duong et al. (2023). Thus, in this paper, we study the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1, which will be subsequently discussed in detail.

Institutional Reward with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1 for the Equally Likely Starting State

In this section, we introduce the analytical results related to the case of institutional reward with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1, when the institution provides reward only when there is a single cooperator in the population. We present the cost function for this particular case together with information on its monotonicity, as well as the limits for the neutral drift and strong selection.

The reward cost function for the threshold value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1 is12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r(\theta )= \frac{N^2\theta }{2} \frac{(W^{-1})_{1,1} + (W^{-1})_{N-1,1}}{N-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er(θ)=N2θ2(W-1)1,1+(W-1)N-1,1N-1,obtained by substituting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1 in (10). Next, we compute explicitly the entries \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(W^{-1})_{1,1}$$\end{document}(W-1)1,1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(W^{-1})_{N-1,1}$$\end{document}(W-1)N-1,1. Note that, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1, the matrix W is a special case of a tri-diagonal matrix of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A=\left( \begin{array}{ccccccc} b_{1} &{} c_{1} &{} &{} &{} &{} &{} \\ a_{2} &{} b_{2} &{} c_{2} &{} &{} &{} &{} \\ &{} \ddots &{} \ddots &{} \ddots &{} &{} &{} \\ &{} &{} a_{j} &{} b_{j} &{} c_{j} &{} &{} \\ &{} &{} &{} \ddots &{} \ddots &{} \ddots &{} \\ &{} &{} &{} &{} a_{n-1} &{} b_{n-1} &{} c_{n-1} \\ &{} &{} &{} &{} &{} a_{n} &{} b_{n} \end{array}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=b1c1a2b2c2⋱⋱⋱ajbjcj⋱⋱⋱an-1bn-1cn-1anbn.We recall the following result from Huang and McColl (1997) that provides analytical formulae for calculating the entries of the inverse matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{-1}$$\end{document}A-1. Theorem 1

(Huang and McColl (1997)) Define the second-order linear recurrences\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} z_{i}=b_{i} z_{i-1}-a_{i} c_{i-1} z_{i-2} \quad i=2,3, \ldots , n \end{aligned}$$\end{document}zi=bizi-1-aici-1zi-2i=2,3,…,nwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_{0}=1, z_{1}=b_{1}$$\end{document}z0=1,z1=b1, and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y_{j}=b_{j} y_{j+1}-a_{j+1} c_{j} y_{j+2} \quad j=n-1, n-2, \ldots , 1 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}yj=bjyj+1-aj+1cjyj+2j=n-1,n-2,…,1where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_{n+1}=1, y_{n}=b_{n}$$\end{document}yn+1=1,yn=bn. The inverse matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{-1}=\left\{ \phi _{i, j}\right\} (1 \leqslant i, j \leqslant n)$$\end{document}A-1=ϕi,j(1⩽i,j⩽n) can be expressed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi _{j, j}=\frac{1}{b_{j}-a_{j} c_{j-1} \frac{z_{j-2}}{z_{j-1}}-a_{j+1} c_{j} \frac{y_{j+2}}{y_{j+1}}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕj,j=1bj-ajcj-1zj-2zj-1-aj+1cjyj+2yj+1where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=1,2, \ldots , n, a_{1}=0, c_{n}=0$$\end{document}j=1,2,…,n,a1=0,cn=0 and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi _{i, j}= {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll}-c_{i} \frac{z_{i-1}}{z_{i}} \phi _{i+1, j} &{} i<j \\ -a_{i} \frac{y_{i+1}}{y_{i}} \phi _{i-1, j} &{} i>j.\end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕi,j=-cizi-1ziϕi+1,ji<j-aiyi+1yiϕi-1,ji>j.

Corollary 1

(Huang and McColl (1997)) The inverse matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{-1}=\left\{ \phi _{i, j}\right\} $$\end{document}A-1=ϕi,j can be expressed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi _{j, j}=\frac{1}{b_{j}-a_{j} c_{j-1} \frac{z_{j-2}}{z_{j-1}}-a_{j+1} c_{j} \frac{y_{j+2}}{y_{j+1}}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕj,j=1bj-ajcj-1zj-2zj-1-aj+1cjyj+2yj+1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=1,2, \ldots , n, \ a_{1}=0$$\end{document}j=1,2,…,n,a1=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{n}=0$$\end{document}cn=0 and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi _{i, j}= {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll}(-1)^{j-i}\left( \prod _{k=1}^{j-i} c_{j-k}\right) \frac{z_{i-1}}{z_{j-1}} \phi _{j, j} &{} i<j \\ (-1)^{i-j}\left( \prod _{k=1}^{i-j} a_{j+k}\right) \frac{y_{i+1}}{y_{j+1}} \phi _{j, j} &{} i>j.\end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕi,j=(-1)j-i∏k=1j-icj-kzi-1zj-1ϕj,ji<j(-1)i-j∏k=1i-jaj+kyi+1yj+1ϕj,ji>j.

We use the above results to derive the following lemma which provides an explicit formula for the reward cost function for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1.

Lemma 1

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1, we have13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r(\theta ) = \frac{N^2\theta }{2(N-1)} \Big (1+d^{N-2}\frac{1}{y_2}\Big )\frac{1}{1-ad\frac{y_3}{y_2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er(θ)=N2θ2(N-1)(1+dN-21y2)11-ady3y2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_2$$\end{document}y2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_3$$\end{document}y3 are found from the following backward recursive formula:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y_{N}= & {} 1,\quad y_{N-1}=1, \quad \text {and}\quad y_{N-i} = y_{N-i+1} - (bd)y_{N-i+2},\quad \text {for}\quad \nonumber \\ i= & {} 1,\ldots , N-2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}yN=1,yN-1=1,andyN-i=yN-i+1-(bd)yN-i+2,fori=1,…,N-2.

Proof

We compute the two entries \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{-1}_{1,1}$$\end{document}W1,1-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{-1}_{N-1,1}$$\end{document}WN-1,1-1 appearing in (12) using Theorem 1. We recall that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W=\begin{pmatrix} 1&{}-a&{}&{}&{}&{}&{}\\ -c&{}1&{}-a&{}&{}&{}&{}&{}\\ &{}\ddots &{}\ddots &{}\ddots &{}&{}&{}\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W=1-a-c1-a⋱⋱⋱-c1-a-d1-b⋱⋱⋱-d1-b-d1,with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a:= & {} (1+e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta )})^{-1}, \quad b:=(1+e^{-\beta \delta })^{-1}, \quad c:=(1+e^{\beta (\delta +\theta )})^{-1}, \quad \\ d:= & {} (1+e^{\beta \delta })^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a:=(1+e-β(δ+θ))-1,b:=(1+e-βδ)-1,c:=(1+eβ(δ+θ))-1,d:=(1+eβδ)-1.In particular, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W=\begin{pmatrix} 1&{}-a&{}&{}&{}&{}&{}\\ -d&{}1&{}-b&{}&{}&{}&{}&{}\\ &{}\ddots &{}\ddots &{}\ddots &{}&{}&{}\\
