We observe that the entries of Table 1 are the binomial coefficients offset by a factor of k for every column and are alternating in sign. This suggests that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j = \sum \limits _{k=0}^{j} (-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(bd)^k$$\end{document}y^j=∑k=0j(-1)kj-kk(bd)k. In the lemma below, we prove this is indeed the case. We also obtain another expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j$$\end{document}y^j using the general formula of a second order homogeneous recurrence relation.

Lemma 2

(Recurrence relation) The following formulae hold for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\ge 0$$\end{document}j≥0\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j= \sum \limits _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k} (bd)^k$$\end{document}y^j=∑k=0⌊j2⌋(-1)kj-kk(bd)k,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j = \frac{x_{1} x_{2}\left( x_{1}^{j-1}-x_{2}^{j-1}\right) -\left( x_{1}^{j}-x_{2}^{j}\right) }{x_{2}-x_{1}}$$\end{document}y^j=x1x2x1j-1-x2j-1-x1j-x2jx2-x1,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x_{1,2}=\frac{1 \pm \sqrt{1-4 b d}}{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x1,2=1±1-4bd2.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j= \sum \limits _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k} (bd)^k$$\end{document}y^j=∑k=0⌊j2⌋(-1)kj-kk(bd)k,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j= \sum \limits _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k} (bd)^k$$\end{document}y^j=∑k=0⌊j2⌋(-1)kj-kk(bd)k,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j = \frac{x_{1} x_{2}\left( x_{1}^{j-1}-x_{2}^{j-1}\right) -\left( x_{1}^{j}-x_{2}^{j}\right) }{x_{2}-x_{1}}$$\end{document}y^j=x1x2x1j-1-x2j-1-x1j-x2jx2-x1,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j = \frac{x_{1} x_{2}\left( x_{1}^{j-1}-x_{2}^{j-1}\right) -\left( x_{1}^{j}-x_{2}^{j}\right) }{x_{2}-x_{1}}$$\end{document}y^j=x1x2x1j-1-x2j-1-x1j-x2jx2-x1,

Proof

We prove the first statement by induction on j. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j = 2$$\end{document}j=2 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_2 = \hat{y}_1-(b d) \hat{y}_0 = 1 - bd$$\end{document}y^2=y^1-(bd)y^0=1-bd. We assume the statement holds for j and need to prove it for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j+1$$\end{document}j+1. In fact, we have15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{y}_{j+1}&=\hat{y}_{j}-(b d) \hat{y}_{j-1} \nonumber \\&=\sum _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(b d)^{k}-(b d) \sum _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j-1}{2}\rfloor }(-1)^k {j-1-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(b d)^{k} \nonumber \\&=\sum _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor }(-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(b d)^{k}-\Bigg (\sum _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j-1}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^k {j-1-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(b d)^{k+1}\Bigg ) \nonumber \\&=\sum _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(b d)^{k}-\sum _{k=1}^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^{k-1} {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k-1}(b d)^{k} \nonumber \\&= 1 +\sum _{k=1}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor }\Bigg ((-1)^k {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k}-(-1)^{k-1} {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k-1}\Bigg )(b d)^{k} \nonumber \\&\quad - (-1)^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor - 1} {j- \lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor - 1} (bd)^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } \nonumber \\&= 1 +\sum _{k=1}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor }(-1)^k \Bigg ({j-k \atopwithdelims ()k} + {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k-1}\Bigg )(b d)^{k} \nonumber \\&\quad + (-1)^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } {j- \lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor - 1} (bd)^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } \nonumber \\&= 1 +\sum _{k=1}^{\lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor }(-1)^k {j+1-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(b d)^{k} + (-1)^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } {j- \lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor - 1} (bd)^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } \nonumber \\&=\sum _{k=0}^{\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } (-1)^k {j+1-k \atopwithdelims ()k}(b d)^{k} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y^j+1=y^j-(bd)y^j-1=∑k=0⌊j2⌋(-1)kj-kk(bd)k-(bd)∑k=0⌊j-12⌋(-1)kj-1-kk(bd)k=∑k=0⌊j2⌋(-1)kj-kk(bd)k-(∑k=0⌊j-12⌋(-1)kj-1-kk(bd)k+1)=∑k=0⌊j2⌋(-1)kj-kk(bd)k-∑k=1⌊j+12⌋(-1)k-1j-kk-1(bd)k=1+∑k=1⌊j2⌋((-1)kj-kk-(-1)k-1j-kk-1)(bd)k-(-1)⌊j+12⌋-1j-⌊j+12⌋⌊j+12⌋-1(bd)⌊j+12⌋=1+∑k=1⌊j2⌋(-1)k(j-kk+j-kk-1)(bd)k+(-1)⌊j+12⌋j-⌊j+12⌋⌊j+12⌋-1(bd)⌊j+12⌋=1+∑k=1⌊j2⌋(-1)kj+1-kk(bd)k+(-1)⌊j+12⌋j-⌊j+12⌋⌊j+12⌋-1(bd)⌊j+12⌋=∑k=0⌊j+12⌋(-1)kj+1-kk(bd)k,where, to obtain (15), we used Pascal’s identity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k} + {j-k \atopwithdelims ()k-1} = {j+1-k \atopwithdelims ()k} \quad \text {for} \quad 1\le k \le \lfloor \frac{j}{2}\rfloor \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j-kk+j-kk-1=j+1-kkfor1≤k≤⌊j2⌋and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {j+1-\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } = {j - \lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor } + {j-\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor - 1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j+1-⌊j+12⌋⌊j+12⌋=j-⌊j+12⌋⌊j+12⌋+j-⌊j+12⌋⌊j+12⌋-1,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${j-\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{j+1}{2}\rfloor - Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lfloor \frac{j-1}{2} \rfloor + 1 = n+1= \lfloor \frac{j+1}{2} \rfloor $$\end{document}⌊j-12⌋+1=n+1=⌊j+12⌋ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=2n+1$$\end{document}j=2n+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lfloor \frac{j-1}{2} \rfloor + 1 = n = \lfloor \frac{j+1}{2} \rfloor $$\end{document}⌊j-12⌋+1=n=⌊j+12⌋ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=2n$$\end{document}j=2n.

For the proof of the second statement, we note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_{j}=\hat{y}_{j-1}-(b d) \hat{y}_{j-2}$$\end{document}y^j=y^j-1-(bd)y^j-2 is a second order homogeneous recurrence relation and we solve it using the characteristic equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x^{2}-x+b d=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x2-x+bd=0.Recall \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=\frac{1}{1+e^{-\beta \delta }}$$\end{document}b=11+e-βδ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=\frac{1}{1+e^{\beta \delta }}$$\end{document}d=11+eβδ, so\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} b d&=\frac{1}{\left( 1+e^{\beta \delta }\right) \left( 1+e^{-\beta \delta }\right) } \\&=\frac{1}{2+e^{\beta \delta }+e^{-\beta \delta }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}bd=11+eβδ1+e-βδ=12+eβδ+e-βδ.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{\beta \delta }+e^{-\beta \delta } \ge 2 \sqrt{e^{\beta \delta } e^{-\beta \delta }}=2$$\end{document}eβδ+e-βδ≥2eβδe-βδ=2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b d < \frac{1}{4}$$\end{document}bd<14 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\text {as }\beta , \delta \ne 0)$$\end{document}(asβ,δ≠0).

Therefore, the characteristic equation has two real solutions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x_{1,2}=\frac{1 \pm \sqrt{1-4 b d}}{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x1,2=1±1-4bd2.Thus, we can express \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_i$$\end{document}y^i using these roots as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{y}_{j}=r_{1} x_{1}^{j}+r_{2} x_{2}^{j}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y^j=r1x1j+r2x2j,where the two constants are found from the initial data:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{y}_{0}=r_{1}+r_{2}=1 \quad \text {and}\quad \hat{y}_{1}=r_{1} x_{1}+r_{2} x_{2}=1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y^0=r1+r2=1andy^1=r1x1+r2x2=1.Solving the above system for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_1, r_2$$\end{document}r1,r2 results in\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} r_{1}=\frac{x_{2}-1}{x_{2}-x_{1}},\quad r_{2}=\frac{1-x_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}r1=x2-1x2-x1,r2=1-x1x2-x1.Hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \hat{y}_{j}&=\frac{x_{2}-1}{x_{2}-x_{1}} x_{1}^{j}-\frac{x_{1}-1}{x_{2}-x_{1}} x_{2}^{j} \\&=\frac{x_{1}^{j} x_{2}-x_{1} x_{2}^{j}-\left( x_{1}^{j}-x_{2}^{j}\right) }{\left( x_{2}-x_{1}\right) } \\&=\frac{x_{1} x_{2}\left( x_{1}^{j-1}-x_{2}^{j-1}\right) -\left( x_{1}^{j}-x_{2}^{j}\right) }{x_{2}-x_{1}}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y^j=x2-1x2-x1x1j-x1-1x2-x1x2j=x1jx2-x1x2j-x1j-x2jx2-x1=x1x2x1j-1-x2j-1-x1j-x2jx2-x1.This completes the proof of the lemma. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The following theorem, which is the main analytical result of the present paper, provides an explicit formula for the reward cost function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ) and shows that it is always non-decreasing for all parameter values.

Theorem 2

(Derivative and monotonicity of the cost function) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r'(\theta )$$\end{document}Er′(θ) is always increasing with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ for all values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N,\theta ,\beta $$\end{document}N,θ,β, where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r'(\theta )=\frac{N^2}{(2N-2)}\Big (y_2+d^{N-2}\Big )\Big (\frac{1}{y_2-ady_3}\Big )\Big [1+\frac{\theta dy_3\beta e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta ) }a^2}{y_2-ad y_3}\Big ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er′(θ)=N2(2N-2)(y2+dN-2)(1y2-ady3)[1+θdy3βe-β(δ+θ)a2y2-ady3].As a consequence, the minimisation problem (6) has a unique solution\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min \limits _{\theta \ge \theta _0} E_r(\theta )= E_r(\theta _0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}minθ≥θ0Er(θ)=Er(θ0).

Proof

Recalling from Lemma 1 that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r(\theta )= \frac{N^2\theta }{2N-2}(y_2+d^{N-2})\Big (\frac{1}{y_2-ady_3}\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er(θ)=N2θ2N-2(y2+dN-2)(1y2-ady3).Next, we compute the derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ) with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ.

Note that only a depends on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a=a(\theta )=(1+e^{-\beta (\theta +\delta )})^{-1}, \quad a'(\theta )=\frac{\beta e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta )}}{(1+e^{-\beta (\theta +\delta })^2} =\beta e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta )} a^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a=a(θ)=(1+e-β(θ+δ))-1,a′(θ)=βe-β(δ+θ)(1+e-β(θ+δ)2=βe-β(δ+θ)a2,while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=(1+e^{-\beta \delta })^{-1}$$\end{document}b=(1+e-βδ)-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=(1+e^{\beta \delta })^{-1}$$\end{document}d=(1+eβδ)-1 (and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_2$$\end{document}y2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_3$$\end{document}y3) do not depend on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ.

Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C=C(\theta )=\frac{1}{y_2-ady_3}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C=C(θ)=1y2-ady3.Then the derivative of C with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C'(\theta )&=\frac{dy_3 a'(\theta )}{(y_2-ad y_3)^2}\nonumber \\&=\frac{d y_3 \beta e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta )} a^2}{(y_2-ad y_3)^2}\nonumber \\&=\frac{d y_3 \beta e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta )} a^2}{(y_2-ad y_3)}C(\theta )\nonumber \\&= \frac{y_{3} \beta e^{- \beta \left( \delta + \theta \right) }}{\left( 1 + e^{- \beta \left( \delta + \theta \right) }\right) ^{2} \left( y_{2} - \frac{y_{3}}{\left( 1 + e^{- \beta \left( \delta + \theta \right) }\right) \left( 1+ e^{\beta \delta }\right) }\right) ^{2} \left( 1 + e^{\beta \delta }\right) }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C′(θ)=dy3a′(θ)(y2-ady3)2=dy3βe-β(δ+θ)a2(y2-ady3)2=dy3βe-β(δ+θ)a2(y2-ady3)C(θ)=y3βe-βδ+θ1+e-βδ+θ2y2-y31+e-βδ+θ1+eβδ21+eβδ.We calculate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E'_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er′(θ) via the product rule:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_r'(\theta )&=\frac{N^2}{2N-2}(y_2+d^{N-2})\Big [C(\theta )+\theta C'(\theta )\Big ]\\&=\frac{N^2}{2N-2}(y_2+d^{N-2})\Big [1+\frac{\theta dy_3\beta e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta ) }a^2}{y_2-ad y_3}\Big ]C(\theta ) \\&= \frac{N^2}{(2N-2)}\Big (y_2+d^{N-2}\Big )\Big (\frac{1}{y_2-ady_3}\Big )\Big [1+\frac{\theta dy_3\beta e^{-\beta (\delta +\theta ) }a^2}{y_2-ad y_3}\Big ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Er′(θ)=N22N-2(y2+dN-2)[C(θ)+θC′(θ)]=N22N-2(y2+dN-2)[1+θdy3βe-β(δ+θ)a2y2-ady3]C(θ)=N2(2N-2)(y2+dN-2)(1y2-ady3)[1+θdy3βe-β(δ+θ)a2y2-ady3].From the formula of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ), it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_2-ad y_3>0$$\end{document}y2-ady3>0.

We next show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_{2}>0$$\end{document}y2>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_{3}>0$$\end{document}y3>0. Recalling that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_2=\hat{y}_{N-1}$$\end{document}y2=y^N-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_3=\hat{y}_{N-2}$$\end{document}y3=y^N-2, where the sequence of numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\hat{y})_{j}$$\end{document}(y^)j is given in Lemma 2. We show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_j>0$$\end{document}y^j>0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\ge 0$$\end{document}j≥0.

In fact, according to the second statement of Lemma 2, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{y}_j&= \frac{x_{1} x_{2}\left( x_{1}^{j-1}-x_{2}^{j-1}\right) -\left( x_{1}^{j}-x_{2}^{j}\right) }{x_{2}-x_{1}} \\&= \frac{x_1x_2(x_1-x_2)\sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-2} x_1^kx_2^{j-2-k}-(x_1-x_2)\sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-1} x_1^kx_2^{j-1-k}}{x_2-x_1} \\&= \sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-1} x_1^kx_2^{j-1-k} - \sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-2} x_1^{k+1}x_2^{j-1-k} \\&= \sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-2} x_1^kx_2^{j-1-k} + x_1^{j-1}x_2^0 - \sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-2} x_1^{k+1}x_2^{j-1-k} \\&= \sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-2} (x_1^kx_2^{j-1-k} - x_1^{k+1}x_2^{j-1-k}) + x_1^{j-1} \\&= \sum \limits _{k=0}^{j-2} x_1^kx_2^{j-1-k}(1-x_1) + x_1^{j-1} >0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y^j=x1x2x1j-1-x2j-1-x1j-x2jx2-x1=x1x2(x1-x2)∑k=0j-2x1kx2j-2-k-(x1-x2)∑k=0j-1x1kx2j-1-kx2-x1=∑k=0j-1x1kx2j-1-k-∑k=0j-2x1k+1x2j-1-k=∑k=0j-2x1kx2j-1-k+x1j-1x20-∑k=0j-2x1k+1x2j-1-k=∑k=0j-2(x1kx2j-1-k-x1k+1x2j-1-k)+x1j-1=∑k=0j-2x1kx2j-1-k(1-x1)+x1j-1>0,as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_1,x_2 \in (0,1)$$\end{document}x1,x2∈(0,1). Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_2, y_3>0$$\end{document}y2,y3>0. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r'(\theta )>0$$\end{document}Er′(θ)>0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta >0$$\end{document}θ>0. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Asymptotic Limits

We now study the neutral drift and strong selection limits of the reward cost function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1 for the equally likely initial state when the intensity of selection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β tends to 0 and to +\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\infty $$\end{document}∞, respectively.

Proposition 1

(Neutral drift limit) It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow 0}E_r(\theta )=\frac{N^{2} \theta }{N-1} \Bigg (P(N)+\frac{1}{2^{N-2}}\Bigg )\Big (\frac{2}{4P(N)-Q(N)}\Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→0Er(θ)=N2θN-1(P(N)+12N-2)(24P(N)-Q(N)),where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(N)&= 1+(-1)^1{N-2-1 \atopwithdelims ()1}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^1+(-1)^2{N-2-2 \atopwithdelims ()2}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{2}+\ldots \\&\quad +(-1)^{\left\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\right\rfloor }{N-2-\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\rfloor }\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\rfloor },\\ Q(N)&= 1+(-1)^{1}{N-3-1 \atopwithdelims ()1}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^1+(-1)^{2}{N-3-2 \atopwithdelims ()2}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{2}+\ldots \\&\quad + (-1)^{\left\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\right\rfloor }{N-3-\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\rfloor }\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\rfloor }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(N)=1+(-1)1N-2-11(14)1+(-1)2N-2-22(14)2+…+(-1)N-22N-2-⌊N-22⌋⌊N-22⌋(14)⌊N-22⌋,Q(N)=1+(-1)1N-3-11(14)1+(-1)2N-3-22(14)2+…+(-1)N-32N-3-⌊N-32⌋⌊N-32⌋(14)⌊N-32⌋.

Proof

Proposition 2

(Strong selection limit) It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty }E_r(\theta )={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} +\infty \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta > 0,\\ \frac{2 N^2\theta }{N-1}\quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta = 0,\\ \frac{N^2\theta }{N-1} \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta < 0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→∞Er(θ)=+∞ifδ+θ>0,2N2θN-1ifδ+θ=0,N2θN-1ifδ+θ<0.

Proof

We proceed as in the proof of the previous proposition by computing the limit of relevant quantities as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}β→∞ instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}β→0, noting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta <0$$\end{document}δ<0 in our setting. We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty } d^{N-2}=\lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty }\left( \frac{1}{1+e^{\beta \delta }}\right) ^{N-2}=1,\\&\lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty } (bd)= \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{2+e^{\beta \delta }+e^{-\beta \delta }}= 0,\\&\lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty } (ad)=\lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty } \frac{1}{(1+e^{\beta \delta })(1+e^{-\beta (\theta +\delta )})}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1 \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta > 0,\\ \frac{1}{2}\quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta = 0,\\ 0 \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta < 0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→∞dN-2=limβ→∞11+eβδN-2=1,limβ→∞(bd)=limβ→∞12+eβδ+e-βδ=0,limβ→∞(ad)=limβ→∞1(1+eβδ)(1+e-β(θ+δ))=1ifδ+θ>0,12ifδ+θ=0,0ifδ+θ<0.It also follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty } y_2= 1, \quad \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty } y_3=1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→∞y2=1,limβ→∞y3=1.Putting everything together yields\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\beta \rightarrow 0} E_{r}(\theta )&=\lim _{\beta \rightarrow 0} \Bigg (\frac{N^2\theta }{2N-2}(1+\frac{d^{N-2}}{y_2})\Big (\frac{1}{1-ad\frac{y_3}{y_2}}\Big )\Bigg ) \\ {}&={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} +\infty \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta > 0,\\ \frac{2 N^2\theta }{N-1}\quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta = 0,\\ \frac{N^2\theta }{N-1} \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta < 0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→0Er(θ)=limβ→0(N2θ2N-2(1+dN-2y2)(11-ady3y2))=+∞ifδ+θ>0,2N2θN-1ifδ+θ=0,N2θN-1ifδ+θ<0.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} The numerical results are in accordance with the analytical ones in Proposition 2

The numerical results are in accordance with the analytical ones in Proposition 2

Institutional Reward with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1 for the All-Defector Starting State

In this section, we introduce the analytical results related to the case of institutional reward with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1, when the institution provides reward only when there is a single cooperator in the population. The computation of the entry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(W^{-1})_{1,1}$$\end{document}(W-1)1,1 is identical to the one in Sect. 3.1, that is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(W^{-1})_{1,1} = \Big ({1-ad\frac{y_3}{y_2}}\Big )$$\end{document}(W-1)1,1=(1-ady3y2). Asymptotic Limits

We now study the neutral drift and strong selection limits of the reward cost function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1 for the all-defector initial state when the intensity of selection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β tends to 0 and to +\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\infty $$\end{document}∞, respectively.

Proposition 3

(Neutral drift limit) It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow 0}E_r(\theta )=\frac{N^{2} \theta }{N-1} \Big (\frac{4P(N)}{4P(N)-Q(N)}\Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→0Er(θ)=N2θN-1(4P(N)4P(N)-Q(N)),where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(N)&= 1+(-1)^1{N-2-1 \atopwithdelims ()1}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^1+(-1)^2{N-2-2 \atopwithdelims ()2}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{2}+\ldots \\&\quad +(-1)^{\left\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\right\rfloor }{N-2-\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\rfloor }\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{\lfloor \frac{N-2}{2}\rfloor },\\ Q(N)&= 1+(-1)^{1}{N-3-1 \atopwithdelims ()1}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^1+(-1)^{2}{N-3-2 \atopwithdelims ()2}\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{2}+\ldots \\&\quad + (-1)^{\left\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\right\rfloor }{N-3-\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\rfloor \atopwithdelims ()\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\rfloor }\Big (\frac{1}{4}\Big )^{\lfloor \frac{N-3}{2}\rfloor }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(N)=1+(-1)1N-2-11(14)1+(-1)2N-2-22(14)2+…+(-1)N-22N-2-⌊N-22⌋⌊N-22⌋(14)⌊N-22⌋,Q(N)=1+(-1)1N-3-11(14)1+(-1)2N-3-22(14)2+…+(-1)N-32N-3-⌊N-32⌋⌊N-32⌋(14)⌊N-32⌋.

Proof

Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta ) = \frac{N^2\theta }{N-1} \Big (\frac{1}{1-ad\frac{y_3}{y_2}}\Big )$$\end{document}Er(θ)=N2θN-1(11-ady3y2). Following similar lines as in the proof of Proposition 1, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\beta \rightarrow 0} E_{r}(\theta )&=\lim _{\beta \rightarrow 0} \Bigg (\frac{N^2\theta }{N-1}\Bigg (\frac{1}{1-ad\frac{y_3}{y_2}}\Bigg )\Bigg ) \\&=\frac{N^{2} \theta }{N-1} \left( \frac{1 }{1-\frac{1}{4}\frac{Q(N)}{P(N)}}\right) \\&= \frac{N^{2} \theta }{N-1} \Big (\frac{4P(N)}{4P(N)-Q(N)}\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→0Er(θ)=limβ→0(N2θN-1(11-ady3y2))=N2θN-111-14Q(N)P(N)=N2θN-1(4P(N)4P(N)-Q(N)).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proposition 4

(Strong selection limit) It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow \infty }E_r(\theta )={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} +\infty \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta > 0,\\ \frac{2 N^2\theta }{N-1}\quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta = 0,\\ \frac{N^2\theta }{N-1} \quad \text {if}\quad \delta +\theta < 0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→∞Er(θ)=+∞ifδ+θ>0,2N2θN-1ifδ+θ=0,N2θN-1ifδ+θ<0.

Proof

Institutional Reward with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 2$$\end{document}t=2

Numerical Analysis

In this section, we present the results of our numerical analysis, coded in Python 3.10. We employ a logarithmic scale to respond to skewness of larger values.

Behaviour of the Cost Functions Under Small Mutation with Different Initial States

In Figs. 5 and 6, we plot the reward cost function for DG for the two initial state assumptions: the dynamics randomly commencing in the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0 or the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN and it starting in the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}S0. Figures 10 and 11 in Appendix, show the behaviour of the reward cost function for PGG, while Figs. 12 and 13 show that of the punishment cost function for DG. In Figs. 14 and 15, we plot the behaviour of the hybrid cost function for DG. The numerical simulations concern some small values of N and various values of the other parameters. From these plots, we observe that the cost functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(\theta )$$\end{document}Er(θ), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_p(\theta )$$\end{document}Ep(θ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{mix}(\theta )$$\end{document}Emix(θ) are increasing for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t < N-1$$\end{document}1≤t<N-1 and become non-monotonic for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = N-1$$\end{document}t=N-1, exhibiting a phase transition. This behaviour is dependant on changes in the strength of selection, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β. The larger this parameter is, the more pronounced the non-monotonic behaviour of the function becomes. The aforementioned behaviour is robust to changes in the game-specific values such as B, c, r, n.

In Figs. 7, 8 and 9, we plot the reward cost function (as a function of the per capital cost \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta )$$\end{document}θ) for the evolutionary processes with general mutation and different starting states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0, S_{\frac{N}{2}}, S_{N-1}$$\end{document}S0,SN2,SN-1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=N-1$$\end{document}t=N-1 (for some N), and various values of other parameters. These figures clearly show the strong and non-trivial impact of the threshold t, the mutation rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ, and the strength of selection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β on the cost function. We observe that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1, the cost function is decreasing with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ. How to prove this interesting phase transition phenomena for general N is elusive to us at the moment and deserves further investigation in the future.

All the simulations in Sect. 5 can be found in the ‘Evolutionary Game Theory’ repository under the ‘Reward and Punishment - General t’ folder.

Discussion

Over the past decades, there has been a lot of attention given to studying effective incentive mechanisms with the aim of promoting cooperation. Various mechanisms have been tested (Nowak 2006b; Sigmund 2010; Perc et al. 2017; Rand and Nowak 2013; Van Lange et al. 2014), with some of the most efficient ones being institutional incentives, where there is a central decision-maker in charge of applying them. In our model, we adopt this idea of an external decision-maker and entrust them with providing reward, punishment, or hybrid incentives to players interacting via two cooperation dilemmas, the Donation Game or the Public Goods Game. While other works have examined a comparable setting, relatively few have looked at the question of optimising the overall cost to the institution while maintaining a certain degree of cooperation. Moreover, most studies have focused on the full-invest approach (also known as the standard institutional incentive model), in which incentives are always provided regardless of the population composition.

In this paper, we studied the problem of optimising the cost of institutional incentives that are provided conditionally on the number of cooperators in the population (namely, when less than a threshold t) while guaranteeing a certain level of cooperation in a well-mixed, finite population of selfish individuals. We use mathematical analysis to derive the cost function as well as the neutral drift and strong selection limits for the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 1$$\end{document}t=1 and the cost function for the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 2$$\end{document}t=2 (using two different initial states). We provide numerical investigation for the aforementioned cases as well as for the others, i.e., for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le t < N-1$$\end{document}2≤t<N-1. We also introduced cost functions with a general mutation rate for reward, punishment, and hybrid incentives and numerically analysed their behaviour.

For the mathematical analysis of the reward incentive cost function to be possible, we made some assumptions. Firstly, in order to derive the analytical formula for the frequency of cooperation, we assumed a small mutation limit (Rockenbach and Milinski 2007; Nowak et al. 2004; Sigmund 2010). Despite the simplified assumption, this small mutation limit approach has wide applicability to scenarios which go well beyond the strict limit of very small mutation rates (Zisis et al. 2015; Hauert et al. 2007; Sigmund et al. 2010; Rand et al. 2013; Duong et al. 2023). If we were to relax this assumption, the derivation of a closed form for the frequency of cooperation would be intractable. Secondly, we focused on two important cooperation dilemmas, the Donation Game and the Public Goods Game. Both have in common that the difference in average payoffs between a cooperator and a defector does not depend on the population composition. This special property allowed us to simplify the fundamental matrix of the Markov chain to a tri-diagonal form and apply the techniques of matrix analysis (Huang and McColl 1997) to obtain a closed form of its inverse matrix. In games with more complex payoff matrices such as the general prisoner’s dilemma and the collective risk game (Sun et al. 2021), this property no longer holds (e.g., in the former case, the payoff difference, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _C(i)-\Pi _D(i)$$\end{document}ΠC(i)-ΠD(i), depends additively on i) and the technique in this paper cannot be directly applied. In these scenarios, we might consider other approaches to approximate the inverse matrix, exploiting its block structure.

We intend to utilise analytical techniques to explore the optimisation problems of punishment and hybrid incentives (reward and punishment used concurrently) for the individual-based incentive scheme. This would be interesting because it would allow for a cost comparison between reward or punishment incentives for a certain threshold value t and the mixed scheme for the same value of t. Finally, there has been little attention given to the use of analysis for obtaining insights into cost-efficient incentives in structured populations or in more complex games (such as the general prisoner’s dilemma and the collective risk game), so this would also be an engaging research avenue.
